- Every run that starts a saved workflow allows `Skill(<name>)` next to `Workflow(<name>)`, and the denial canary allows `Skill(<name>)` without `Workflow(<name>)`; why: a saved workflow is also in the session's skill listing and `/<name>` reaches the model as an instruction, so the model sometimes calls the `Skill` tool instead of `Workflow`; with only the `Workflow` rule that call is denied and the run ends `success`, exit 0, with nothing started; seen in 2 of 21 launches on 2026-11-13, both among the 8 launches of `kit-probe-hook` (canary 3 `inconclusive` in the third full run, and 1 of 5 repeats, where `permission_denials` names `Skill`); with the rule, canary 3 held in 8 of 8 repeats, one of them through the `Skill` route; checked by hand on 2.1.323: with both rules the `Skill` route reaches the workflow agent, and with `Skill(<name>)` alone the `Workflow` call is still denied, so `Workflow(<name>)` stays the gate and the `PreToolUse` hook on `Workflow` sees either route; consequence for fact 4 and D4 item 1: the CI allow list names both rules for each workflow a job may start, otherwise a launch can do nothing and still exit 0; alternative: take the `Skill` tool away with `--disallowedTools`, not tested; revisit if: step 2.4 removes the `Skill` tool from CI sessions, or the CLI starts a named workflow without a model turn.
- The seventh canary (the owner's addition, 2026-11-13) holds: a saved workflow starts by name and its agent runs with `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_BACKGROUND_TASKS=1` in settings `env`. The same run still ends in two result messages (`result_index` 1), exactly as without the setting, so the setting does not turn a workflow launch into a single-result run; consequence for D4 item 3: "background tasks off" cannot by itself protect a consumer that stops at the first result, so step 0.3 tests the action with a workflow and not only with a background subagent; revisit if: step 0.3 shows the action waits for the second result.
- Side observations on 2.1.323 that later steps rest on (in `observed` of the record): `PreToolUse` fires for the `Workflow` tool in the main session with the workflow name in `tool_input.name` (step 1.2, D7); a workflow agent's `SubagentStart` carries the main session's id, so the fan-out total can count it (step 1.2); settings `env` values reach hook processes, which is how canaries 5 and 7 show that a setting was in force; a hook block in the main session or in an ordinary subagent is listed in the result's `permission_denials`, and the same block inside a workflow agent is not (canary 4: `denied_tools` names `Bash`; canary 3: `blocked_rows` 1 next to an empty `denied_tools`), so the result JSON alone does not show what a workflow agent was refused (step 2.4).
- Skeleton vs production-grade after step 0.1: the probe is run by hand; the scheduled run, the issue on a changed canary and a compare mode are step 3.1; the `kit.manifest` entry is step 2.5; the canary for a timed-out hook is step 1.1; no canary forces the `Skill` route to a workflow, the two hand checks are its evidence until step 2.4 fixes the CI allow list; an `inconclusive` canary is re-run by hand with `--only`, with no automatic retry; revisit if: the scheduled run of step 3.1 turns out noisy.

## 2026-11-15 — orchestration and CI plan rev 3: five controls and two modes (docs/PLAN-2026-11-orchestration-ci.md)

- Kacper approved the direction of rev 3 on 2026-11-15 by answering four questions (Polish, verbatim): "1 tak, 2, tak, 3 mozna lokalnie, lub to gemini ktore juz mielismy, albo dokupie suba openai, 4. zostaje przez tozszamosc bota, i niech to dziala jak tam w zalozeniach, planujemy razem itp, a potem ai i agneic dostarczaja az osiagna cel". In order: (1) write rev 3 with the five controls and the two modes; (2) resume step 0.2 in the wider scope, first; (3) second reviewer: a local model, the Gemini he already has, or an OpenAI subscription; (4) AI attribution stays, through the bot identity; and the three stated defaults stand (own private repositories may be open, with the egress screen before any second vendor; the first merge class is docs and tests after 20 trial changes with no verifier miss; cloud workers start through `claude --cloud`, not Projects). Later that day, shown the step plan for 0.2 and two points where rev 3 differed from those defaults (the switch is a `mode` key, not `boundary`; the merge class is narrower than "docs and tests"), he answered (verbatim): "tak, zgadzam sie i dowiez mi to prosze". The text was then reviewed three times and changed again; what he has not yet seen is the plan's list "Changed by the review of 2026-11-15", in which Q10 and Q13–Q15 need his answer, and the text of rev 3 is waiting for his review.
- Plan rev 3 follows a second research round (`pstack`, `dark-factory`, `lauren-poteto-rules`). `pstack` is not installed: it is prose with no hooks or CI, and its defaults ("Don't wait for confirmation", cloud workers, the owning agent merging, 14 or more verification agents, a PR watcher that does not look for an approval) conflict with this kit; five of its ideas become checks; cost: about 1 working day on top of rev 2, no running cost; compliance: each idea is adopted as a test or a CI check, not as an instruction; revisit if: `pstack` ships enforcement this kit lacks.
- Rev 3 was reviewed three times before the owner read it (2026-11-15). First review: four lenses with distinct jobs (compliance, consistency, facts, GitHub mechanics) and one batched verifier; 39 findings, 7 blockers; the verifier checked the 32 that would change the design and confirmed every one. All were addressed; the remedies of three were replaced: the deploy and delete asks (left to the owner as Q14), the class switch (a committed line, not an environment variable) and step 2.6 (a base-branch job, not a reminder). Second check, of the rewrite: one reviewer found 32 findings resolved, 3 partly and 4 deliberately not taken, and one tried to break the new design; 13 findings, 2 blockers, all addressed. Last check, of that second round: one reviewer on closure and one on the truth of the new claims; 11 findings, none a blocker, all addressed, and the author's own re-count of the merge class took its yield from 2 of 60 to none. The changes of this last round were checked by the author only, and no further round was run; why: the first draft promised controls the code did not have (a new target inherited `boundary: own`; an uncommitted edit could open a repository; a cloud session acting as the owner could approve its own change; a pull request could post the gate's own status), and each rewrite added text the round before had not seen; cost: about 985,000 subagent tokens and 23 minutes in all; compliance: the findings and verdicts are in the session's workflow journals and not in the repository, so this line and the plan's list of changes are the record; revisit if: never.
- A worker brief names its done-evidence (command and expected result), its writable and forbidden paths, its forbidden actions and its report fields, and a linter rejects an empty field; a worker's result is data checked by `assert_run.py` (branch, head commit at the branch tip, exit code per check, a not-verified list); a missing result is a gap, never a pass; the first packet runs end to end before the rest; why: a worker's summary is prose, and the tool reports success for a run that did nothing or was denied (facts 2 and 3 of the plan); compliance: validation evidence per packet, bound to the exact commit; revisit if: never.
- Every `AGENTS.md` rule gets a row in `docs/RULES.md` that names its enforcer or marks it as judgment, with a static test that the enforcer exists; a judgment-only rule broken twice gets a check or a dated exception; why: a researched repository wrote "Authors do not certify themselves." in its rules while its own gap list records "All merges self-merged, 0 reviews"; compliance: an auditor can read which controls are technical and which are procedural; revisit if: the table and the README section "What is enforced where" disagree twice (then one is generated from the other).
- A pull request that touches the guard's configuration, hooks, workflows or settings fails a required check unless it adds a `DECISIONS.md` line; the check runs from the base branch and reads the changed paths through the API, so the PR cannot remove it; why: rule 4 of the working agreement was enforced only by an `ask` on the laptop, and a check defined in the PR's own `ci.yml` could be deleted by the PR it judges; compliance: change control for the controls themselves, visible in the PR; revisit if: it blocks more than two mechanical changes a month (then an explicit marker with a reason).
- Panel findings state their evidence kind (command run, quoted line, inference); the verifier re-checks the first two; unconfirmed findings are listed and counted, not dropped; a PR's title, body and comments are data in every CI job and never part of a shell command; why: a dropped finding and a refuted one look the same in a summary, and PR text is written by whoever opens or comments on the PR; revisit if: never.
- Two modes per repository, strict by default (Kacper, 2026-11-15, translated: "when a project is secret, no; if it is not secret, use all resources"): `mode: open` is honoured only when the file committed on the default branch says it together with `boundary: own`; the working tree, a local override and the environment cannot open a repository; the installer writes `mode: strict` and `boundary: client` into a new target unless `--boundary own` is given, which changes today's default (a target used to inherit this repository's `own`); that installer default is Q13 of the plan and waits for the owner's answer. Strict = the plan as approved in rev 2 plus the five controls; open adds cloud workers, a budget in place of the spawn count, reviewers from other model families, and merge for one narrow class after a trial. A key of its own, not `boundary` as first proposed, because `boundary` says whose data it is, an own repository can still be secret, and the guard's default `boundary` is `own`. In both modes force-push is denied and an `ask` in a cloud session is a `deny`; deploys and deletions ask the owner, who can silence those asks through `rules_off` only in a strict own repository (the knob of 2026-11-01), never under `mode: open` (the default of Q14, which waits for the owner's answer); cost: about 4 working days for Phase 4, no running cost for the switch itself; compliance: the regime of a repository is one reviewed line on its protected branch, and a new client repository starts closed without anyone remembering to close it; the guard that reads the line is a file in the working tree, so it stops a mistake and a session that keeps to its tools, not one that rewrites refs or the guard, which is a named residual; revisit if: a client asks for a cloud feature inside its boundary.
- Strict-mode hardening found by the review of rev 3 (steps 1.7 and 1.8), built in Phase 1 whether or not any repository is opened: `KZ_BOUNDARY` can only tighten (today `KZ_BOUNDARY=own` switches `egress-guard` off in a repository whose file says `client`); a guard rule denies every way a session can start cloud work, because `claude --cloud` uploads the local repository with the history of all branches when the vendor's App is not installed on it; in a cloud session every `ask` is a `deny`, `rules_off` is ignored and `gh pr merge` is denied; an edit under `.github/workflows/` asks, and `guard-config` also covers shell writes to its paths; the guard denies a read of the owner's stored GitHub credentials and the commands that rewrite the refs it reads, as far as matching command text can go; the installer's default boundary becomes `client` (Q13, not yet answered); why: each of these was a way for a client repository to end up looser than its owner believed; cost: a little over half a working day by the plan's sizes (step 1.7 is M + S and step 1.8 is S, where S is under an hour and M about half a day), no running cost; compliance: client data cannot reach a cloud session through an agent's command that the guard sees, and the boundary of a new repository fails closed; a session that rewrites the guard or its refs is a named residual (D11), and for that case the judgment controls of D12 carry the protection; a person starting a cloud session by hand stays a judgment control, written down as one in `docs/RULES.md`; revisit if: never for the tightening; the installer default if own repositories turn out to be the common install target.
- Cloud workers, open mode only = `claude --cloud`, one single-repository session per packet; Projects threads and multi-repository sessions are not used, because they read no repository hooks (docs, read 2026-11-15). They are used only if probe 4.1 shows that the guard hook fires, that an `ask` is not approved on its own, and that the session cannot commit, comment, review or merge as the owner, push a workflow file or reach another repository; the docs already say Auto-fix replies under the owner's GitHub account, and the owner's approval is a comment by that account, so Auto-fix stays off. Under `mode: open` the guard lets through only `claude --cloud` from a local session, on a pushed branch whose hook configuration and hook scripts equal the default branch's, because a cloud session runs the hooks of the branch it clones; routines, remote agents and launches from inside a cloud session stay denied. Cloud workers need the required checks of step 2.2 first, since the proxy does not restrict branches. GitHub is connected through the vendor's App on open repositories only (the scratch repository of probe 4.1 is the one exception, for the probe), and `/web-setup` is not used while the `gh` token reaches a strict repository. A repository is opened only after a secret scan of its full history, because a cloud session receives all of it, and the vendor's retention and training terms are quoted here before the first worker runs; cost: $0 extra a month on the subscription, since usage draws on the plan's limits; the API-equivalent is unknown until probe 4.1 shows where a session's cost is visible, and the local ledger, spawn log and guard decision log cannot see it; compliance: unavailable to a client repository by rule, as far as the guard holds (D11), for agent-issued launches and by judgment for human ones, and every cloud fact here is docs-only until probe 4.1; revisit if: the probe fails any of the conditions (then cloud workers wait).
- Open mode bounds a fan-out by money, not by head count: `fan-out` is listed in `rules_off` (spawns are still logged), every headless run carries `--max-budget-usd` from `run_budget_usd`, and the ledger flags a session above the line; the flag is checked against Claude Code's own estimate and can be passed, so the figure is set with headroom; there is no hard cap inside an interactive session, so on a subscription the bound is the plan's limits; why: a head count is a poor proxy for cost; cost: $15 for a packet run and $60 for a session as first lines, API-equivalent, guesses until step 0.2 has a month of data; per month no extra money on the subscription, and for scale the last 30 days were about $1,790 API-equivalent across all projects; compliance: the spawn log and the ledger keep the record a count would have given; revisit if: API billing replaces the subscription (a live cap comes first), or a session passes twice the line.
- Second reviewer (settles Q8; Kacper, 2026-11-15: a local model, the Gemini he already has, or an OpenAI subscription): one adapter interface behind the egress screen; order: the local model, then Gemini through `agy`, an OpenAI model only if those two prove too weak over 20 panel runs. The local model is inside the boundary and so allowed in strict mode, which widens the line "Local llama-server only as a `judge` backend during fleet batches" (its trigger, an item class that must stay on the machine, has fired). In strict mode the adapter refuses any target outside `egress_allowed_targets`, whatever the boundary says. The second reviewer is a panel lens and not the verifier of the merge gate, which runs in CI. Gemini returns as a reviewer of diffs; the Gemini review of plans stays suspended; why: a reviewer from the same model family shares the author's blind spots; cost: $0 a month for the first two; an OpenAI subscription, roughly $20 to $200 a month by tier as last known to the author and to be confirmed on the vendor's page, is not bought; compliance: a diff leaves the machine only after the egress screen, and each outside vendor's retention and training terms are quoted here before its first run; revisit if: over 20 runs the second lens confirms nothing the Claude lenses missed.
- Verifier and merge, open mode only: every agent-authored PR gets a verdict (`pass`, `fail`, `gap`) per head SHA from a run that did not write the change. Two workflows: the evidence on `pull_request` with no secret and commands fixed on the base branch; the verdict on `workflow_run` from the default branch, in a token environment that accepts `main` only; the evidence artifact is data and is never executed. The verifier is the same model family as the author, so its worth is what the trial shows: for 20 changes the owner approves each one and the verdict is recorded next to his decision, bound to one model and version. After 20 with no miss he switches on the class by a committed line. The class is per repository; here it is new, undecorated test functions in `test_*.py` files of existing `tests/` directories and nothing else (none of the last 60 changes on `main` would have met it; "docs and tests" read widely, 9 of 60), and no document; never instruction or control documents, plans, `DECISIONS.md`, `README.md`, anything under the `guard-config` rule, anything `kit.manifest` installs, CI, IaC, `conftest.py`, test configuration, or a diff that changes an existing test line (narrower than the "docs and tests" accepted on 2026-11-15; the class is the default of Q10, which has three answers and waits for the owner's). `merge-gate` is a required check computed from the base branch, and the gate merges at the SHA it judged, so no agent decides a merge, and a local session that runs a merge command can merge only what the required checks already allow. After the switch the owner samples 1 in 5 merged changes; one miss, or a month without a sample, switches the class off. Two rev 2 lines change for an open repository: the ruleset's required approvals go back from 1 to 0 there, with `merge-gate` carrying the approval, and the Q1 deviation widens to a second job that uses the token environment without a label (both are the default of Q15, which waits for the owner's answer); why: the owner does not want to verify every change himself (2026-11-15), and a verdict is worth what its evidence is worth; the trial measures honest changes and not an author who writes for the verifier; cost: about $0.4–1.4 a PR API-equivalent by four measured `fresh-review` runs ($0.38 to $1.37; two jobs may cost more until measured), so about $10–30 a month at 20 agent-authored PRs and $0 in money on the subscription; with the panel this can pass the $20-a-month trigger of the review line of 2026-11-13, which is then the owner's to move; compliance: the record names who or what approved each head SHA, and strict mode keeps the owner's approval for every change; on a personal repository a required check is pinned to its source app and not to a workflow, so the gate rests on no agent identity being able to push a workflow or post a status; revisit if: one miss, or a forged check is ever seen (then a second App posts the gate).
- AI attribution stays in both modes, carried by the agent identity (Kacper, 2026-11-15); the `Co-Authored-By` trailer stays on every agent commit in strict mode, and in open mode it may be dropped only on a commit whose author is the GitHub App (step 2.1); cloud-session commits always keep it; hiding that an agent wrote a change is not done in either mode; compliance: Attributable (ALCOA+); revisit if: never.
- Named residual, both modes (second check of rev 3): an approval by the owner's login is only as strong as keeping his credentials out of a session. The approval check counts a review or an `/approve <head-sha>` from a non-agent user with write access and ignores agent identities, and sessions act as the App; but the owner's own `gh` token and git credentials stay on the machine where local sessions run, and a guard that matches command text can be evaded. Detection has the same shape: it reads the committer e-mail, which a session sets itself, and the login that opened the PR, so a session commit that carries the owner's e-mail, in a PR he opened by hand, passes as human work. Lines of defence: sessions use the App's token and identity by default; the guard denies reading the owner's credentials, posting an approval and committing under another identity; the structural fix, sessions that run without those credentials (a sandbox, a container or a separate OS user), is deferred; why named and not fixed now: the fix changes how every local session runs and deserves its own plan; cost: none for naming it, and the fix is not estimated; compliance: Attributable (ALCOA+) holds for approvals only under this assumption, and an auditor should be told so; revisit if: a denied credential read appears in the guard's decision log, or the owner asks for sessions in containers (he raised container-only installs on 2026-11-15).
- Step 0.2 is widened and built first: the ledger reads workflow-agent transcripts, prices 1-hour cache writes, stores Claude Code's own figure as the session cost next to its own count, and a probe canary fails when the two differ by more than 2 % on a probe session; why: over 30 days the ledger saw $491 of the $1,538 in the transcripts ($1,793 by Claude Code's figure), and both the panel's cost figure (step 1.4) and the open-mode budget (step 4.4) need it; cost: about half a working day and about $0.04 a probe run; compliance: a cost record that states its basis; revisit if: Claude Code documents the `cost-state` line or ships a file exporter for its telemetry.
- Skeleton vs production-grade after rev 3: everything in Phase 4 rests on docs-only facts until probe 4.1, and cloud workers may turn out unusable there; the budget lines are guesses; the merge class is a first version that none of this repository's last 60 changes would have met; whether a personal repository can keep `pull_request_target` after 2026-12-02 is unconfirmed, with a fallback named in step 2.2; a person starting a cloud session by hand is a judgment control; the owner's approval rests on his credentials staying out of a session's reach, and the guard is a file a session can rewrite, both named residuals; a live cap inside an interactive session, lessons from transcripts, a wider merge class, a gate posted by a second App, a verifier from another model family, sessions that run without the owner's credentials and carrying a verdict across a rebase are not built (deferred, each with its trigger).

## 2026-11-15 — orchestration step 0.2: the ledger sees the whole cost (#38)

- The ledger reads every `agent-*.jsonl` under `<session>/subagents/` at any depth, so the workflow agents in `subagents/workflows/wf_<id>/` count next to ordinary subagents; `journal.jsonl` and the `*.json` meta files are skipped; the row keeps `subagents.transcripts` (all agent transcripts) and adds `subagents.workflow_transcripts`; why: the old glob read only the first level, and over 30 days the files it did not read held about $1,048 of the $1,538 the transcripts are worth at list price; compliance: a cost record that leaves out two thirds of the spend is not a complete record (ALCOA+); revisit if: Claude Code moves or renames agent transcripts (the canary of step 0.2 then fails or goes inconclusive).
- `prices.json` as of 2026-11-15 from the published API price list: a `cache_write_1h` rate in every row, non-null rows for every model id seen in the last 30 days of transcripts (`claude-opus-5-5`, `claude-sonnet-5`, `claude-sonnet-5-5`, `claude-haiku-5-5`, `claude-haiku-4-5`, `claude-fable-5-1`, `claude-fable-5-2`) and the four older rows kept; `claude-sonnet-5-5` has its own row because its cache reads cost half of `claude-sonnet-5`'s; Haiku 5.5 carries a `long_prompt` tier and each request whose prompt (input + cache read + cache write) is over 100,000 tokens is priced at it; the ledger counts 1-hour writes per model as `cache_write_1h` (a part of `cache_write`), a write with no 5m/1h breakdown and every row written before this change are priced at the 5-minute rate; why: the main session writes its cache for 1 hour and agents write theirs for 5 minutes (over the 30-day set all 19.6 million cache-write tokens of the main transcripts were 1-hour writes and none of the 96.9 million in agent transcripts, 17 % in all; the shares by model, 88-100 % on Fable and 13 % on Opus 5.5, only show which model ran as the main session), a 1-hour write costs 1.6 times the 5-minute rate, so pricing it adds $82 or 5.4 % to the 30-day own count, all of it on main transcripts, and six of the seven model ids in use had no price (three null rows, two missing rows, and `claude-sonnet-5-5` caught by the null `claude-sonnet-5` prefix); known gap: fast mode (2x) and US-only inference (1.1x) are not priced, and the usage object already carries `speed` and `inference_geo` per request (over the last 30 days `speed` was `standard` or absent and `inference_geo` was `global`, `not_available` or absent), so they can be priced later; cost: none; compliance: the price source and its date are in the file, so each figure can be re-derived; revisit if: the price list changes, a new model id appears with `cost_usd: null`, or `speed` shows anything other than `standard`.
- The session row records Claude Code's own figure next to the ledger's count: `cost_usd_own` (the ledger's count), `cost_usd_cc` (the sum of the last `cost-state` snapshot of each `startTime` in the main transcript), `cost_basis` (`claude-code` or `own`) and `cost_check` (`ok`, `missing`, `out-of-range`, `unknown-model-cost`, `unchecked`, and `error` on the fallback row the hook writes when building the row raises, with basis `own` and no figures); `cost_usd` keeps its name and holds the figure of the basis, so existing readers keep working; Claude Code's figure is used when it lies between 98 % and 150 % of the own count, otherwise the own count, and any check other than `ok` marks the session; two cases the brief left open are decided here: `hasUnknownModelCost` true falls back to the own count (`unknown-model-cost`), and with no own count to check against (a model without a price) Claude Code's figure is used and marked `unchecked`, so a new model still gets a figure that is visibly unverified; a `cost-state` line with a missing or non-numeric `totalCostUSD` is ignored and counted in `parse_errors`; `ledger.py cost` prints sessions per basis and the marked ones per check; why: in interactive sessions 14-20 % of the cost is in requests that never reach a transcript, so Claude Code's figure is the closer one, but once it carried 62.68 USD over into a session worth 1.21 USD, so it is only trusted inside a band around a count the ledger can re-derive; cost: none; compliance: every session cost states its basis and why, and both figures stay in the row; revisit if: Claude Code documents the `cost-state` line or ships a file exporter for its telemetry, or more than a tenth of the sessions of a month are marked.
- The probe gets an eighth canary, `ledger-own-cost`: the ledger's own count over the transcripts of the `workflow-by-name` probe run, its workflow agent included, against that run's `total_cost_usd`; `holds` within 2 %, `fails` outside, `inconclusive` when the run was not clean, when the transcript, a workflow-agent transcript, a price or the cost field is missing, when the cost field or the own count is not a finite number, when a transcript has parse errors, or when the ledger cannot be loaded or raises while counting (the reason names the exception type); it judges a run the probe already pays for and adds none of its own, and it finds the transcript by the run's session id under the Claude config directory (inconclusive too when more than one project folder holds a file with that id); first live result on 2026-11-15 on CLI 2.1.323: 8 of 8 canaries hold, and the own count was 5,357 micro-USD against 5,357 reported by the run, 1 basis point apart before rounding; why: the ledger's count is only worth recording if something fails when Claude Code moves agent transcripts, changes how usage is written or a model's price changes, and a `claude -p` run is where the two counts are known to match; cost: none beyond the probe run, which stays at about $0.04; compliance: the check that the cost record is complete is itself a dated record in `verified.json`, with the CLI version it ran on; revisit if: the canary is inconclusive on two runs in a row (the transcript lookup or a price row then needs attention).
- Measured after the build, on 2026-11-16, over the 30-day set (every main transcript changed on or after 2026-10-16, all projects, with its agent transcripts; 79 sessions; the session that was still running is left out), at list price: the ledger's own count is $1,502.17, of which $429.78 in main transcripts, $24.74 in subagents and $1,047.65 in workflow agents (363 agent transcripts, 316 of them workflow agents, no parse error), and the files the old code read hold $454.52 of it; a reviewer's script that does not import the ledger gives the same $1,502.17, and the same $1,792.89 for Claude Code's figure; under the basis rule the session costs total $1,791.28, with 77 sessions on Claude Code's figure and 2 marked ones on the own count: one without a `cost-state` line ($59.85) and one with a carried-over $62.68 against an own count of $1.21; why it is recorded: this is the step's acceptance check, the own count matches what the transcripts hold, and it is the first month of data for the budget lines of the plan's Q11; the $1,538 and $491 measured on 2026-11-15 included the running session, about $36 of main-transcript usage, and that is the whole difference; on the 77 unmarked sessions the own count is $1,441.11 against Claude Code's $1,730.22, 17 % lower, which is the usage that never reaches a transcript; compliance: every figure can be re-derived from the transcripts and the price file of 2026-11-15; revisit if: the two counts of a later month differ by more than a fifth, or more than a tenth of its sessions are marked.
- Skeleton vs production-grade after step 0.2: production-grade are the reading of every agent transcript, the 1-hour cache-write rate, the price rows for the seven model ids in use, the basis rule with its checks, and the canary, all with tests on Python 3.9 and 3.13; skeleton: `prices.json` is a hand-kept snapshot of one date; fast mode and US-only inference are not priced; requests that never reach a transcript are covered only through Claude Code's figure, which rests on the undocumented `cost-state` line, so after a CLI change every session may fall back to the own count (the marks in `ledger.py cost` would show it); rows written before 2026-11-15 are not recomputed, so a report over a past period mixes old, lower figures with new ones until those rows age out; the canary compares the two counts on one small `claude -p` session, not on an interactive one; `ledger.py cost` still totals the own count per model and prints no total of the session costs.

## 2026-11-16 — installs stay off the developer machine

- An agent installs nothing on the developer machine (`AGENTS.md`, security reflexes): no package or runtime installer and no fetch-and-run command (`npx <pkg>`, `uvx <tool>`, `curl … | sh`); checks run offline from the local cache (`uv run --offline`, `npx --no-install`), anything that needs a download runs in a container, and a missing dependency is reported to the owner; why: an installer runs third-party code (install scripts, build back ends) with the owner's rights, on a machine where his GitHub and cloud credentials are readable by any process of his user (the residual named in plan rev 3), and a machine that agents change by hand is not the documented environment the checks claim to run in; approved by the owner on 2026-11-16, after his question of 2026-11-15, when no such rule existed; cost: 0 CHF/month, paid for with a slower loop when a dependency is missing from the cache; compliance: the aim is that the tool set that produces validation evidence changes only through a reviewed image or the owner's own action (ALCOA+ original and attributable); the records do not show that yet: `decisions.jsonl` holds that a prompt was raised (time, rule, tool, session), not the answer and not the package, so the session transcript is the only record of an install the owner approved; skeleton: the container route does not exist yet (no pinned dev image, no wrapper), so today the only compliant answers to a missing dependency are the owner's own install or his yes to a prompt, and neither leaves a record of what was installed; the README's test and lint commands carry `--offline` for the same reason; revisit if: sessions themselves run in a container or a sandbox without the owner's credentials (then the rule applies to the image build, not to each command).
- Guard 0.6.0 adds the rule `host-install`: before a shell command that would install packages or fetch and run code on the machine, the hook asks, in both harnesses; ask and not deny, because the owner may approve a one-off and a deny would also block an install he asks an agent to make; the rule reads command text, so it is a second line of defence against habit, behind the rule in `AGENTS.md`, and it is not a sandbox: an install inside a script, a Makefile or a package script, an implicit download by a build tool (`terraform init`, `cargo build`, `go test`), a file that is downloaded first and run later, and a deliberately disguised command all pass; what it cannot read it asks about instead of passing: a command nested more than five levels deep (`sh -c`, `eval`, `python -m`, a `run` or `exec` word), and an error inside the matcher itself, because an uncaught error ends the hook with exit 1 and both harnesses then run the command with no rule speaking; cost: 0 CHF/month, no network and no subprocess in the hook; compliance: every ask is one line in `decisions.jsonl` (time, rule, tool, harness, session, no command text), and so is every ask the knob or `rules_off` silenced (`skipped`), so prompts and silenced passes stay countable per rule; evidence: the corpus holds 108 ask and 108 allow commands per harness, and two reviewers attacked the first version on 2026-11-16 (of 247 candidate commands 69 differed from the expected verdict, 21 after the fix pass, each a limit named in this block); skeleton: the log holds no answer and no command text; a here-document or here-string fed to a shell (`bash <<'EOF'`), a `function name { … }` body, a Python script and `python -c` are not read; the shared `strip_heredocs` helper drops everything after a here-document whose end marker it misses (an empty body, a tab-indented `<<-` marker, a quoted `<<`), for this rule as for the older ones; `UV_OFFLINE=1 uv run …` asks although it is offline, and a read-only command whose argument is an install word asks (`npm search install`, `brew bundle check`); revisit if: the rule asks weekly and the answer is never no (prompt-fatigue review), or sessions move into a sandbox or a container (then the boundary does the work and this rule is a courtesy).
- What asks: the package and runtime installers of the default stack and their neighbours (`npm`, `pnpm`, `yarn`, `bun`, `pip` and `python -m pip`, `pipx`, `uv`, `poetry`, `pdm`, `pipenv`, `conda`/`mamba`, `brew`, `apt`/`apt-get`, `dnf`/`yum`, `apk`, `snap`, `cargo install|add`, `go install|get` and `go mod download`, `gem`, `bundle`, `mise`, `asdf`, `nvm`, `fnm`, `volta`, `pyenv`, `rustup`, `corepack`, `pre-commit install`, `az extension add`, `az bicep install`, `gh extension install`), by their install words wherever these stand after the tool name (`npm --prefix web install`, `yarn workspace web add zod`), a bare `yarn` or `bundle`, and the npm words that rewrite or rebuild the dependency tree (`audit fix`, `dedupe`, `rebuild`, `link`); the fetch-and-run forms `npx`, `pnpx`, `bunx`, `uvx`, `npm exec`, `npm init <pkg>`, `pnpm dlx`, `yarn dlx`, `go run <pkg>@<version>` and `uv run`, unless the command carries the flag that keeps it on the local cache (`--no-install`, `--no`, `--offline`); `uv run --no-sync` asks, because by uv's own help the flag only skips the project sync and `--offline` is the flag that disables network access; and a downloaded script handed to an interpreter that reads its program from standard input (`curl … | sh`, `curl … | python3 -`, also through `tee`, behind `sudo` or after `2>&1`), `sh -c "$(curl …)"`, `eval $(curl …)` and `bash <(curl …)`, while a pipe into a program of its own passes (`curl … | python3 -m json.tool`, `curl … | jq .`); this is wider than the list the owner approved (npm, pnpm, yarn, pip, pipx, `uv add`, brew, cargo, `go install`, gem, `curl … | sh`): the same kinds of command for more tools, and `uv run` without `--offline` is the one addition that will be felt daily; why: `AGENTS.md` says checks run offline from the cache, and a bare `uv run` or `npx` downloads without saying so; revisit if: the owner wants the narrow list (drop the rows), or a task repo uses an installer outside the table (add a row and two fixtures; known ones outside it: `deno`, `nix`, `pnpm fetch`, `pnpm env use`, `yarn set version`, `uv venv`, `asdf plugin add`, `volta pin`, `python -m ensurepip`).
- What stays silent on purpose: a command whose first word is not in the table is never read past that word, so the container route passes (`docker run … npm ci`, `docker compose run … pip install`, `ssh host '…'`, `kubectl exec … -- …`), also behind a wrapper (`sudo docker run …`, `timeout 600 docker run …`); the rule has its own quote-aware splitter for this, because the older `segments()` cuts `sh -c 'cd /w && npm ci && npm test'` at `&&` and would have asked about an install that runs inside a container (the older rules keep `segments()`; changing them is a separate decision); a command substitution written inside double quotes is judged even in a container command (`docker run img sh -c "x=$(npm ci)"`), because the host shell runs it before the container starts; wrappers (`sudo`, `env`, `timeout`, `xargs`, `find -exec`, …), `sh -c`, `eval`, `python -m` and whatever follows a `run` or `exec` word are followed; here-document bodies are data, as in the other rules; shell comments are dropped, as the shell drops them (a shell that does not treat `#` as a comment, such as an interactive zsh without `interactivecomments`, would run what follows a `;` there, and the rule does not read it); version, help and lookup probes pass (`npx --version`, `pip install --help`, `command -v npx`); revisit if: an agent is seen wrapping an install in a container command that does not run in a container.
- The knob `host_install` is `auto` (default), `ask` or `off`, and `KZ_HOST_INSTALL` overrides it; the engine applies it the way it applies `rules_off`: the rule still runs, and an ask the knob silences is logged as `skipped`; in `auto` the asks are silenced when the guard itself runs in a disposable environment: `/.dockerenv` or `/run/.containerenv` exists (a container), or `GITHUB_ACTIONS` or `TF_BUILD` (a build agent), `CODESPACES` (a cloud dev box), `REMOTE_CONTAINERS` (a VS Code dev container) or `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE` (a Claude Code cloud session) is set to something other than empty, `0` or `false`; a bare `CI` does not count, because it is easy to have on a laptop; why: there the install is the point (CI installs its test tools, a container is where `AGENTS.md` sends installs, and the Copilot cloud agent turns every ask into a deny, so it could never install a dependency); limits: a dev container on the owner's laptop is silent although VS Code forwards his SSH agent and Git credential helper into it, so an installer there still reaches those credentials (the container narrows file access, not that; `/.dockerenv` would silence it even without the variable), and a self-hosted build agent sets the same variables and is long-lived; the signals and the knob are read from the hook's own process, which a command cannot change, but whoever starts the session can set them, under every boundary, client repos included, which `rules_off` does not allow (it needs a committed `boundary: own`); what makes that acceptable is the `skipped` line per silenced ask; `rules_off: ["host-install"]` in the owner's `guard.local.json` silences the rule in an own repo the same way; any other value of the knob asks; compliance: a committed `off` is a reviewed policy change, an `off` from the environment shows only as `skipped` lines, and the fixtures call the rule directly, below the knob, so the corpus gives the same result on a laptop, in CI and in a container; revisit if: a container runtime without those markers is used (set `KZ_HOST_INSTALL=off` in its definition), a long-lived container or build agent should count as a machine to protect (set `ask`), or `skipped` lines for this rule appear in a client repo outside CI.

## 2026-11-16 — gaps closed after step 0.2: the ledger and the probe record

- The ledger marks what it does not price instead of pricing it: a request whose usage says `speed` other than `standard` (fast mode, 2x) or `inference_geo` other than `global` or `not_available` (US-only inference, 1.1x) is counted per value under `unpriced` in the session line (`{"speed:fast": 3}`), over the main transcript and every agent transcript, and `ledger.py cost` prints how many sessions of the period hold such requests; a session without one writes exactly the line it wrote before, with no new key; why: over 15,347 requests in 472 transcript files on 2026-11-16 no request carried either value, so a multiplier would be untested code on the cost path, while a silent under-count is the thing to prevent: the mark says that the own count of the session is too low, and for which kind of request; approved by the owner on 2026-11-16 (mark, do not price); cost: none; compliance: a figure known to be too low says so in the record (ALCOA+ accurate); skeleton: no multiplier is applied; Claude Code's own figure is its own calculation, but a session with many such requests can lift it above the band of the basis rule (150 % of the own count), and the session then falls back to the too-low own count, where it shows as marked (`out-of-range`) and as unpriced; a request is counted before its usage is checked, so one that later counts as a parse error is in the mark too; revisit if: `ledger.py cost` prints the line at all (then price it: 2x on `speed: fast`, 1.1x on US-only inference, from the price list of that day).
- `ledger.py cost` prints the total of the session costs after the table, `total session cost: $1,234.56 over <n> sessions`, and how many lines it left out and why: `unrecorded` (no `cost_basis`: written before 2026-11-15) and `no cost` (a basis but no finite figure); the table above it still totals the own count per project and model; why: the session figure is Claude Code's own when it passes the check, so it is the one closest to the real spend, and the report had no sum of it (named as a gap on 2026-11-15); a total that skipped lines without saying so would read as complete; cost: none; compliance: the total names what it does not cover (ALCOA+ complete); revisit if: the two totals of a month differ by more than a fifth, the trigger already set on 2026-11-15.
- `ledger.py recount` is a one-off for the lines written before 2026-11-15, which carry no `cost_basis` and no cost: a dry run by default, `--write` applies it; a line whose transcript still exists (exactly one `<session id>.jsonl` under the projects folder) is rebuilt with today's reader and prices, keeps its `ts` and its month file, and keeps everything the rebuild replaces under `recount.was` (the old `cost_usd`, `models` with their token counters, `parse_errors`, `spawns`, `subagents`); a line without a transcript stays as it is and is labelled `recount.transcript: missing`; a transcript that yields no model, or fewer tokens than the old line holds, is not evidence, so that line stays too, labelled `incomplete`; byte-identical lines of one session in one month file (the hook of October ran twice for one SessionEnd) are one candidate and become one line, with `recount.lines` holding how many there were, which is the ledger's own rule since step 3.7 (one line per session id); lines of one session that differ, a line without a session id and a `ts` that does not belong in its file are named, left untouched and make the run exit 1; a line that is not a candidate is byte-identical afterwards, and a second run changes nothing; why: transcripts are deleted after 30 days by default and the oldest here are from 2026-10-27, so these figures could be rebuilt only until about 2026-11-26, and a report over a past month mixed lines without a figure with new ones (named as a gap on 2026-11-15); approved by the owner on 2026-11-16; cost: none; compliance: the old figures stay in the line next to the new ones with the date of the recount, a label says why a line has no figure, and nothing is dropped except a byte-identical copy, whose count is recorded (ALCOA+ original and complete, attributable to a dated run); evidence: on a copy of the owner's ledger (38 lines) the dry run wrote nothing, not even the lock file, and the write rebuilt 12 sessions ($1,707.65, each on Claude Code's own figure with check `ok`), labelled 22 as missing, turned one identical pair into one line, left the 3 lines that were no candidates byte-identical and in order, and a second run was a no-op; the run on the ledger itself, on 2026-11-16 after the commit, gave the same result (October 7 lines to 6, November 31 to 31), the two month files as they were before it are kept next to them in `backup-before-recount-2026-11-16/` with their checksums, and `ledger.py cost --days 30` now prints a total of $1,707.66 over 14 sessions with 23 lines left out (22 without a transcript, 1 newer line without a figure); a reviewer attacked the first version with hand-made lines of every kind (it lost no line, but an empty transcript overwrote a line's token counters, which the `incomplete` label now prevents); skeleton: a rebuilt figure is the transcript as it is on the day of the recount (`recount.at`), which for a session that was continued later is more than the line's `ts` suggests; a rewritten line moves to the end of its month file; a hook that writes the same session between the scan and the write is not guarded against; the sessions that have a transcript and no line at all are not backfilled (62 on 2026-11-16, about $61, mostly probe runs); any rewrite of a month file, by the hook as by the recount, would split a hand-made line that holds a raw carriage return (the hook never writes one, and the owner's files hold none); `ledger.py cost` still stops on a line that is not UTF-8; revisit if: a later change of the reader or of the prices should be applied to old lines again (the verb takes only lines without a basis).
- The probe replaces local paths in its record before it writes `verified.json`: the scratch workdir (as given and resolved) becomes `<workdir>`, the system temp folder `<tmp>` and the home folder `~`, the longest path first, in every string at any depth, keys included; the terminal output keeps the real paths, because the operator needs them there; why: a canary's reason can carry exception text or a stderr excerpt, the file is committed to a public repository, and a home path holds the user name; the committed file was clean, and a test now fails if it ever holds `/Users/`, `/home/`, `/private/`, `/var/folders/` or `/tmp/`; cost: none; compliance: the committed record stays evidence of what ran, and on which CLI version, without carrying personal data; skeleton: a plain substring replace, and `execute()` and `cli_version()` cut stderr at 200 characters before the scrub, so a path cut in half would pass; revisit if: a canary records a path on purpose.

## 2026-11-16 — trial: Claude Code's Bash sandbox for sessions in this repository

- Sessions in this repository are to be started with Claude Code's built-in Bash sandbox, as a trial: `claude --settings ~/.claude/sandbox-trial.json` from the repository root; the file is the owner's, lives outside the working tree (a sandboxed command can write inside the working tree) and is not installed by the kit; why: the `host-install` rule reads command text and only prompts, while the sandbox takes the network and the credential files away from every shell command and its children, whatever the command is called, so it answers the residual named in plan rev 3 and in the block of the same day on installs (credentials readable by any process of the owner's user); approved by the owner on 2026-11-16 as a trial, and it starts when he copies the file and starts a session with it; cost: 0 CHF/month; compliance: least privilege for the agent's shell, set from outside the repository: `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false` given with `--settings` makes the sandbox "admin-required" in the documentation's terms (sandboxing page, read 2026-11-16; needs CLI 2.1.285 or later), and the repository's own `excludedCommands`, allowed domains and write paths are then ignored (measured for `excludedCommands`, see the evidence), which matters in a repository where an agent can edit `.claude/settings.json` with the file tools; revisit: after two weeks of use (keep, widen the domain list, or drop), and before the first client repository (there the whole session belongs in a container, not only its shell commands).
- The settings, as tested on CLI 2.1.323 on macOS: `sandbox.enabled: true`, `failIfUnavailable: true`, `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false`, `autoAllowBashIfSandboxed: false`, `excludedCommands: ["git commit *", "gh *", "python3 tools/tracker/tracker.py *"]`, `network.allowedDomains: ["github.com"]` with `network.strictAllowlist: true`, `filesystem.allowWrite: ["~/.cache/uv"]`, and `credentials.files` with `deny` for `~/.ssh`, `~/.gnupg`, `~/.kube`, `~/.config/gh`, `~/.config/sops`, `~/.codex`, `~/.docker/config.json` and `~/Library/Keychains`; why each: `autoAllowBashIfSandboxed: false` so that a sandboxed command still goes through the permission flow (in auto-allow mode it would run without a prompt), set in the file and not left to the default; `strictAllowlist` because the owner's default permission mode is `auto`, and without it a command had a package registry added for one call and the download ran (measured: HTTP 200), with it the request is refused ("the configured sandbox allowlist is the whole allowlist here"); `git commit` is excluded because commits are signed and gpg cannot reach its key inside; `gh` because its TLS setup fails inside the sandbox on macOS, and the tracker because it calls `gh`; the uv cache is writable because `uv run --offline` builds its environment there; the keychain folder is hidden because a lookup of an existing item succeeded inside the sandbox without it.
- Evidence: headless `claude -p` runs on the cheapest model, in scratch folders and one in this repository, about 8 cents in total: both package registries refused (403, the host is not on the allow list), a direct socket refused, `git ls-remote` on github.com works, the hidden folders list as empty and their files cannot be read, a keychain lookup of an existing item reports not found (no secret was read in any test), a subagent and a workflow agent are held by the same sandbox, and in this repository the whole test suite (2,119 passed, 9 skipped), `ruff check tools`, the git reads and `gh` as an excluded command work; a sandboxed command could not open the project's or the user's settings file for writing; and a scratch folder whose own `.claude/settings.json` excluded a command from the sandbox and added a package registry to the allowed domains got neither with the trial file (the command stayed inside and was refused a file in a hidden folder, the registry was refused), while with `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false` left out of the file the same folder setting took that command out of the sandbox, so that one key is what holds the line.
- What it costs in daily work, and what it does not cover: a commit must be a Bash call of its own (`cd … && git commit`, a pipe or a redirection keeps the call inside the sandbox, where signing fails), and signatures cannot be verified inside (`%G?` shows `E`); `gh`, the tracker and `git commit` run with full access, as before; docker does not work inside (its config file is hidden and the daemon is not reachable), so a container route for installs needs docker excluded, and an excluded command runs with full access; a push, and anything else that needs a credential, runs at the `!` prompt or in another terminal; not covered: the file tools, web fetches, hooks and MCP servers run outside the sandbox by design; a sandboxed command can still read `~/.claude` (transcripts and memory) and whatever else in the home folder is not listed, can write the whole working tree, the guard's own source included (`tools/guard/guard.py`; the settings files are protected), and `github.com` on the allow list is a way out for data; skeleton: nothing in the kit installs or checks these settings, the trial is one file on one machine; revisit if: the trial is kept (then the kit ships the file as a template with a test that reads it, and the container route for installs is decided with it).

## 2026-11-16 — guard 0.6.1: the deploy and delete asks read each command

- `deploy-ask` and `delete-guard` judge a shell command twice and ask when either pass asks: the text patterns of 0.6.0, kept byte for byte, and a new token pass over every simple command the shell would run. The reader (`nested_commands`, built on the lexer of `host-install`) follows nested scripts to five levels: `$( … )` and backticks, `sh -c` and its relatives (bash, zsh, dash, ksh, fish, ash, `su -c`, `script -c`, `flock -c`, `$SHELL -c`), a here-string to one of them, `env -S`, `eval`, and the quoted words of `ssh`, `watch`, `parallel`, `trap` and `tmux`. A deploy tool counts wherever its name stands as a word (behind `sudo`, `timeout`, `npx`, `uv run`, `ssh host`), and its verb is found past its options (`terraform -chdir=infra apply`, `kubectl -n prod delete …`); a dry run is judged per call (the last `--dry-run` decides, bare or `client`/`server`/`true`; `--check` for ansible; `validate` as a subcommand word of az). The delete words `rm rmdir unlink trash shred truncate` count only where a command can stand: the first word after assignments and shell words, and what a wrapper there runs (`sudo -u bob rm`, `timeout 5 rm`, `xargs rm`, `find … -exec rm`, `ssh host rm`, `time`, `flock`, `chroot`, `script`), followed through chains of wrappers, so `grep -rn rm .` and `sudo grep -rn rm /etc` stay silent; why: measured on 0.6.0, 15 of 44 delete forms and 8 of 17 deploy forms asked, and a variable, a wrapper or an option in front of the verb was enough to pass without a prompt (`AWS_PROFILE=prod terraform apply`, `timeout 600 terraform apply`, `sudo -u root rm -rf build`, `kubectl -n prod delete pod x`); now all 61 ask; approved by the owner on 2026-11-16, for these two rules only; cost: none (0 CHF/month); compliance: the guard is the change-control gate of the agent's shell, every ask is logged in `decisions.jsonl` with the rule, the verdict, the session and the time, and a deploy or a deletion the owner did not see is what that log exists to rule out (the line holds no guard version: which version judged a command follows only from the commit checked out at that time, a gap in the record that is named here and not closed); revisit if: a deploy or delete runs without a prompt in a form this block does not name (add the form as a failing test first).
- Both rules fail closed where 0.6.0 failed open: a nest deeper than five levels, more than 32 tool names or 64 command positions in one simple command, and any error inside either rule now ask (an uncaught error meant exit 1 before, which both harnesses let run; a script name holding a NUL byte did that at 0.6.0); why the caps: every tool name and every command position may read the rest of the line, and the first build needed 5.5 s for a 99 kB line (0.19 s at 0.6.0), past the hook's 5 s timeout, which fails open in both harnesses; now the new code adds at most 0.43 s to a line of 96 to 100 kB (0.64 s against 0.21 s at 0.6.0 in the worst case of 37 long inputs), and an everyday command takes 0.03 s as before.
- How it was built and checked: one Opus engineer built it from a brief of this session; two Opus reviewers with different jobs (one hunted for commands that pass without a prompt, one for regressions against 0.6.0, noise, cost and crash paths) returned 21 findings (2 blockers, 7 major, 12 minor); a second Opus engineer closed them, test first. The first build had replaced the text pass of `deploy-ask`: of the 112 inputs the review left behind, 68 got the wrong verdict and 17 that asked at 0.6.0 no longer did, which is why the text pass came back and stays. Evidence in the suite: all 112 review inputs as tests; a frozen copy of the 0.6.0 rule as an oracle over 11,550 generated commands (0.6.0 asks on 9,915, and 0.6.1 asks on every one of them with the same label); 250 new fixtures from both harnesses; timing tests on the six slowest inputs of the first build. After the fix pass this session ran a held-out check with 298 forms the engineer never saw: none that asked at 0.6.0 stopped asking, 11 were still silent (as at 0.6.0), and 8 of those were closed in the same change by the session itself, test first (a here-string without a space, `env -S'…'`, a script that starts with a dash, `time { … }` and `coproc { … }`, BSD `script file command`, and the quoted words of `parallel`, `trap` and `tmux`). Over the 2,158 distinct Bash commands of 73 real session transcripts of this project no verdict changed in `deploy-ask`, `delete-guard` or `host-install`, so nothing that was actually run gets a new prompt. Suite: 2,403 passed, 9 skipped on Python 3.13 and 3.9.
- What it does not cover (measured, and silent at 0.6.0 too; the guard reads text and prompts, it is no sandbox): a tool or verb held in a variable or an alias (`$TF apply`); a deploy started by a script, by `make deploy` or by an interpreter one-liner (`deploy-ask` does not look into scripts; `delete-guard` does, through its text patterns); a here-document or a pipe fed to a shell, beyond a plain `rm` at the start of a line; `make VAR='…'`; `--dry-run` or `--check` given as the value of another option counts as a dry run; a delete inside a container (`docker run -v "$PWD":/w alpine rm -rf /w/build`, `docker exec web rm …`, `docker compose exec …`), behind a runner (`uv run rm`, `poetry run rm`, `mise exec -- rm`) or as `busybox rm`; and the verbs the tables never had, 31 of them checked (among them `docker image|container|network rm`, `docker buildx --push`, `kubectl label|annotate|set image|run|taint`, `terraform workspace delete`, `terraform force-unlock`, `git checkout <ref> -- <path>`, `aws s3api delete-*`, `aws ecs update-service`, `aws lambda update-function-code`, `gh run rerun`, `argocd`, `flux reconcile`, `qm start|migrate`, `pct`, `skopeo|crane|podman push`, `az webapp deploy`, `az containerapp update`). Accepted noise, because the other choice is a hole: a deploy or delete tool named with its verb as plain words asks (`echo terraform apply`), and an option without `=` may take the next word as its value, so the word after that counts as a possible command too (`command -p cat rm` asks). Skeleton: the tables restate the 0.6.0 patterns tool by tool; the only additions are the spellings `docker-compose` and `pip3.x`; revisit if: a container or runner form shows up in real use (then the owner decides which of them ask, because `docker run --rm …` is also the route for installs).
- Found by the review outside the approved scope, measured and left as they are until the owner says otherwise: `git-guard`, `tracker-guard`, `secret-shell` and `protected-branch` still read a line through `segments()` and miss the forms this change closes for the other two (`timeout 30 git commit --no-verify -m x`, `bash -c 'git push origin main'`, `timeout 5 gh issue close 3`, `bash -c 'cat .env'`); `host-install` lets 21 of 22 wrapper and nesting forms pass that the new reader finds (`su -c`, `$SHELL -c`, `bash <<<`, `bash -c -e`, `env -S`, a leading redirection); `segments()` is quadratic (a line of `sudo -a` takes 1.9 s through the hook at 96 kB and 7.4 s at 192 kB, at 0.6.0 and now) and the `find` and `xargs` text patterns backtrack (24 kB of `xargs` takes 1.3 s, 156 kB of `find . -exec` 8.4 s), so a long line of that kind runs past the 5 s timeout and passes unjudged, by every rule; revisit: on the owner's yes, the same reader for those rules and a linear `segments()`.

## 2026-11-17 — the six open branches go to review as stacked pull requests (#41–#46)

- The six local branches were pushed and opened as six pull requests, each on the branch below it: #41 `docs/plan-orchestration-ci` on `main`, #42 `feat/orchestration-probe`, #43 `docs/plan-orchestration-rev3`, #44 `feat/ledger-full-cost`, #45 `feat/guard-host-install`, #46 `fix/guard-ledger-gaps`; why stacked: every branch is built on the one below, so a pull request against `main` would show the lower branches' changes again and a reviewer could not tell which pull request owns a line; approved by the owner on 2026-11-16 (push the six branches, open the tracker issue for the install rule, open the six pull requests); the session opened issue #40 for the install rule, and the bodies say `Refs`, never a closing word, because closing an item is the owner's (`owner ready` for #37, #38 and #40 waits for him); merging is his, from the bottom up; cost: none; compliance: each change now has its own reviewable record with the checks of that head (ALCOA+ attributable and contemporaneous: the pipeline run and the review belong to one commit); revisit if: a stack this deep has to be rebased after a review (then merge sooner, two or three branches deep at most).
